\begin{remarks}[5.9.12]
\label{IV.5.9.12}
\begin{enumerate}[{\rm(i)}]
  \item\label{IV.5.9.12.i}
    Let $\boldsymbol{C}(X)$ be the category of $\sh O_X$-Modules, and let
    $\boldsymbol{C}_Z(X)$ be the subcategory of $\boldsymbol{C}(X)$ formed by
    the $\sh O_X$-Modules with support contained in $Z$; this subcategory
    is localizing in the sense of Gabriel, and the functor
    $\sh H^0_{X/Z}$ is none other than Gabriel's localization functor
    (cf.~\textup{[27]}; this would provide another proof of
    \sref{IV.5.9.11}).  When $Z$ is closed, the functor
    $i^*:\boldsymbol{C}(X)\to\boldsymbol{C}(X\setmin Z)$ (where
    $i:X\setmin Z\to X$ is the canonical injection) defines an
    \emph{equivalence} of categories
    \[
      \boldsymbol{C}(X)/\boldsymbol{C}_Z(X)\approx\boldsymbol{C}(X\setmin Z).
    \]
  \item\label{IV.5.9.12.ii}
    It follows from \sref{IV.5.9.11} that the condition
    $\sh H^0_{X/Z}(\sh F)=0$ is \emph{equivalent} to
    $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh F)\subset Z$.  Indeed, it implies the latter,
    since the kernel of $\rho_{X/Z}$ is then equal to $\sh F$.
    Conversely, if $\operatorname{Supp}(\sh F)\subset Z$, it suffices to
    apply the second part of \sref{IV.5.9.11} to the unique homomorphism
    $u:\sh F\to0$ to conclude that the corresponding homomorphism
    $v:\sh H^0_{X/Z}(\sh F)\to0$ is an isomorphism.
  \item\label{IV.5.9.12.iii}
    The preceding developments keep a meaning for every locally
    Noetherian ringed space in which every closed irreducible subset
    has exactly one generic point.  In particular, on a locally
    Noetherian prescheme they apply to arbitrary \emph{sheaves of abelian
    groups} (regarded as Modules over the simple sheaf associated with
    the constant presheaf $\mathbf Z$).  For every $x\in X$ one still has
    the canonical isomorphism \eqref{IV.5.9.6.1}, in which
    $\widetilde{\sh F}_x$ denotes the sheaf \emph{induced} on the
    subspace $X_x$ of $X$ by the sheaf $\sh F$; the direct proof follows
    immediately from the definition \eqref{IV.5.9.1.2} and the theorem
    on double inductive limits.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remarks}

\subsection{Property \texorpdfstring{$(\mathrm{S}_2)$}{(S2)} and
\texorpdfstring{$Z$}{Z}-closure}
\label{subsection:IV.5.10}

\begin{env}[5.10.1]
\label{IV.5.10.1}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme and let $\sh F$ be a
coherent $\sh O_X$-Module.  For every subset $T$ of $X$, put
\end{env}

\begin{equation}
\label{IV.5.10.1.1}
\operatorname{prof}_T(\sh F)
 =\inf_{z\in T}\operatorname{prof}(\sh F_z)
\tag{5.10.1.1}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[5.10.2]
\label{IV.5.10.2}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, let $Z$ be a subset of
$X$ stable under specialization, and let $\sh F$ be a
quasi-coherent $\sh O_X$-Module.  The following conditions are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[{\rm a)}]
  \item $\sh F$ is $Z$-pure.
  \item $\operatorname{Ass}(\sh F)$ does not meet $Z$.
\end{enumerate}
If, in addition, $\sh F$ is coherent, these conditions are also
equivalent to:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item[\rm c)] $\operatorname{prof}_Z(\sh F)\geq1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
To say that $\sh F$ is $Z$-pure means that, for every Noetherian
open subset $V$ of $X$ and every open subset $U\supset X\setmin Z$, the
restriction homomorphism $\Gamma(V,\sh F)\longrightarrow\Gamma(V\cap U,\sh F)$
is injective \sref{IV.5.9.8}.  By \sref{IV.3.1.8}, this is
equivalent to
$V\cap\operatorname{Ass}(\sh F)\subset U$, which proves the
equivalence of \emph{a)} and \emph{b)}.  Further, to say that
$x\in\operatorname{Ass}(\sh F)$ means that no element of
$\mathfrak m_x$ is $\sh F_x$-regular \sref{IV.3.1.2}; when
$\sh F$ is coherent, this is equivalent to
$\operatorname{prof}(\sh F_x)=0$.  The equivalence of \emph{b)} and
\emph{c)} follows immediately.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[5.10.3]
\label{IV.5.10.3}
Let $0\longrightarrow\sh F'\longrightarrow\sh F
\longrightarrow\sh F''\longrightarrow0$
be an exact sequence of quasi-coherent $\sh O_X$-Modules.  If
$\sh F$ is $Z$-pure, then so is $\sh F'$.  Conversely, if $\sh F'$
and $\sh F''$ are $Z$-pure, then so is $\sh F$.
\end{corollary}
----- English, components/10a_section510_s2_z_closure_through_corollary5109.tex, page IV-2 115 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{115}

\begin{proof}
This follows from the form \sref{IV.5.10.2}, \emph{b)}, of the
condition for a quasi-coherent $\sh O_X$-Module to be $Z$-pure,
and from \sref{IV.3.1.7}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[5.10.4]
\label{IV.5.10.4}
Suppose that $\sh F$ is coherent.  For $\sh F$ to be $Z$-pure at
a point $x\in X$, it is necessary and sufficient that $\operatorname{prof}_{Z_x}(\widetilde{\sh F}_x)\geq1$,
with the notation of \sref{IV.5.9.6}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This follows immediately from \sref{IV.5.10.2} and
\sref{IV.5.9.6}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[5.10.5]
\label{IV.5.10.5}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, let $Z$ be a subset of
$X$ stable under specialization, and let $\sh F$ be a coherent
$\sh O_X$-Module.  For $\sh F$ to be $Z$-closed, it is necessary
and sufficient that $\operatorname{prof}_Z(\sh F)\geq2$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By \sref{IV.5.10.2}, we may restrict ourselves to the case in
which $\sh F$ is $Z$-pure and
$\operatorname{prof}_Z(\sh F)\geq1$.  Moreover, to say that
$\operatorname{prof}_Z(\sh F)\geq2$ is equivalent to saying that $\operatorname{prof}_{Z_\alpha}(\sh F)\geq2$
for every closed subset $Z_\alpha$ of $Z$; similarly, by
\sref{IV.5.9.8}, to say that $\sh F$ is $Z$-closed is equivalent
to saying that it is $Z_\alpha$-closed for every $\alpha$.  We
may therefore first reduce to the case in which $Z$ is \emph{closed}.

The question is local.  For each $x\in Z$, it is enough to prove
the theorem for $\sh F|U$, where $U$ is an affine open
neighbourhood of $x$; we may thus suppose that $X=U$ is affine.
Then $\operatorname{Ass}(\sh F)$ is finite \sref{IV.3.1.6}, and,
since $\operatorname{Ass}(\sh F)\subset X\setmin Z$, there is a section
$f$ of $\sh O_X$ over $X$ such that
\[
  \operatorname{Ass}(\sh F)\subset X_f\subset X\setmin Z
  \qquad\text{\sref[II]{II.4.5.4}}.
\]
It follows that $f$ is $\sh F$-regular \sref{IV.3.1.9}, and for
every $y\in Z$ one has $f_y\in\mathfrak m_y$, hence
\[
  \operatorname{prof}(\sh F_y)
   =1+\operatorname{prof}(\sh F_y/f_y\sh F_y)
  \qquad\text{\sref[0]{0.16.4.6}}.
\]
Thus $\operatorname{prof}_Z(\sh F)\geq2$ is equivalent to
$\operatorname{prof}_Z(\sh F/f\sh F)\geq1$, or, by
\sref{IV.5.10.2}, to $\sh F/f\sh F$ being $Z$-\emph{pure}.  It remains
to show that this last property is equivalent to $\sh F$ being
$Z$-\emph{closed}.

Consider the exact sequence $0\to\sh F\xrightarrow{f}\sh F
\to\sh F/f\sh F\to0$,
where multiplication by $f$ is injective by hypothesis.  Put
$W=X\setmin Z$.  We have the commutative diagram
\[
\xymatrix{
  0 \ar[r] &
  \Gamma(X,\sh F) \ar[r]^{f} \ar[d] &
  \Gamma(X,\sh F) \ar[r] \ar[d] &
  \Gamma(X,\sh F/f\sh F) \ar[r] \ar[d] &
  0 \\
  0 \ar[r] &
  \Gamma(W,\sh F) \ar[r]^{f} &
  \Gamma(W,\sh F) \ar[r] &
  \Gamma(W,\sh F/f\sh F) &
}
\]
whose two rows are exact, since $X$ is affine.  If the
restriction homomorphism $\Gamma(X,\sh F)\longrightarrow\Gamma(W,\sh F)$
is bijective, the diagram shows that
\[
  \Gamma(X,\sh F/f\sh F)
   \longrightarrow\Gamma(W,\sh F/f\sh F)
\]
is injective; hence $\sh F/f\sh F$ is $Z$-pure
\sref{IV.5.9.8}.

Conversely, suppose that $\sh F/f\sh F$ is $Z$-pure, and let $s$
be a section of $\sh F$ over $W$.  Since $X_f\subset W$, there is
an integer $n>0$ such that $f^n(s|X_f)$ extends to a section $t$
of $\sh F$ over $X$ \sref[I]{I.1.4.1}.  The restrictions of $t$
and $f^ns$ to $X_f$ are equal; the relation
$\operatorname{Ass}(\sh F)\subset X_f$ therefore implies, by
\sref{IV.5.10.2}, that $t|W=f^ns$.  Since $f$ is
$\sh F$-regular, it is enough to show that $t=f^nt'$ for some
$t'\in\Gamma(X,\sh F)$.
----- English, components/10a_section510_s2_z_closure_through_corollary5109.tex, page IV-2 116 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{116}

This is equivalent to the image of $t$ in
$\Gamma(X,\sh F/f^n\sh F)$ being zero.  For every $k$,
$f^k\sh F/f^{k+1}\sh F$ is isomorphic to $\sh F/f\sh F$, hence is
$Z$-pure.  By induction on $n$, using \sref{IV.5.10.3}, it follows
that $\sh F/f^n\sh F$ is $Z$-pure.  But the image of
$t|W=f^ns$ in $\Gamma(W,\sh F/f^n\sh F)$ is zero; the assertion
follows from $Z$-purity.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[5.10.6]
\label{IV.5.10.6}
Let $\sh F$ be a coherent $\sh O_X$-Module.  For $\sh F$ to be
$Z$-closed at a point $x$, it is necessary and sufficient that $\operatorname{prof}_{Z_x}(\widetilde{\sh F}_x)\geq2$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This follows from \sref{IV.5.9.6} and \sref{IV.5.10.5}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[5.10.7]
\label{IV.5.10.7}
\emph{(Hartshorne).}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme and let $Y$ be a closed
subset of $X$.  Suppose that
$\operatorname{prof}(\sh O_{X,y})\geq2$ for every $y\in Y$.
Then, for every connected component $C$ of $X$,
$C\setmin(C\cap Y)$ is connected.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We may restrict ourselves to the case in which $X$ is connected.
By \sref{IV.5.10.5}, the canonical homomorphism $\sh O_X\longrightarrow i_*(\sh O_X|X\setmin Y)$,
where $i:X\setmin Y\to X$ is the canonical injection, is bijective.
Consequently the restriction homomorphism $\Gamma(X,\sh O_X)\longrightarrow\Gamma(X\setmin Y,\sh O_X)$
is also bijective.  It remains to apply
\sref[III]{III.7.8.6.1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[5.10.8]
\label{IV.5.10.8}
Let $X$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme and let $d$ be an
integer such that, for every $x\in X$,
\[
  \dim(\sh O_x)\geq d
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \operatorname{prof}(\sh O_x)\geq2.
\]
Suppose that $X$ is connected.  If $X'$ and $X''$ are two
distinct irreducible components of $X$, there is a sequence
$(X_i)_{0\leq i\leq n}$ of irreducible components of $X$ such
that $X_0=X'$, $X_n=X''$, and
\[
  \codim(X_{i-1}\cap X_i,X)\leq d\setmin 1
  \qquad(1\leq i\leq n).
\]
\textup{In this case one says that $X$ is
\emph{connected in codimension} $\leq d\setmin 1$.}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
If $Y$ is a closed subset of $X$ such that
$\codim(Y,X)\geq d$, then
$\dim(\sh O_{X,y})\geq d$ for every $y\in Y$
\sref{IV.5.1.3}; hence
$\operatorname{prof}(\sh O_{X,y})\geq2$ for every $y\in Y$, and
\sref{IV.5.10.7} shows that $X\setmin Y$ is connected.

For $\codim(Y,X)\geq d$, it is necessary and sufficient that,
for every $y\in Y$, there be an open neighbourhood $V$ of $y$
such that $\codim(Y\cap V,V)\geq d$
\sref[0]{0.14.2.3}.  Let $\mathfrak Y$ be the set of closed
subsets $Y$ of $X$ of codimension at least $d$.  The union of two
members of $\mathfrak Y$ belongs to $\mathfrak Y$
\sref[0]{0.14.2.5}, and every closed subset of a member of
$\mathfrak Y$ also belongs to $\mathfrak Y$.  In other words,
$\mathfrak Y$ is an \emph{antifilter} of closed subsets of $X$.
The corollary now follows from the following topological lemma.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[5.10.8.1]
\label{IV.5.10.8.1}
Let $X$ be a connected locally Noetherian topological space and
let $\mathfrak Y$ be an antifilter of closed subsets of $X$.
Suppose that a closed subset $Y$ belongs to $\mathfrak Y$ whenever,
for every $y\in Y$, there exist an open neighbourhood $V$ of $y$
in $X$ and a $Y_y\in\mathfrak Y$ such that
$V\cap Y=V\cap Y_y$.  The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[{\rm a)}]
  \item For every $Y\in\mathfrak Y$, the space $X\setmin Y$ is connected.
  \item If $X'$ and $X''$ are two distinct irreducible components
  of $X$, there is a sequence $(X_i)_{0\leq i\leq n}$ of
  irreducible components of $X$ such that $X_0=X'$, $X_n=X''$,
  and
  \[
    X_{i-1}\cap X_i\notin\mathfrak Y
    \qquad(1\leq i\leq n).
  \]
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose \emph{b)} holds and let $Y\in\mathfrak Y$.  Put
$U=X\setmin Y$.  If $U'$ and $U''$ are two distinct irreducible
components of $U$, there are irreducible components $X'$ and
$X''$ of $X$ such that
$X'\cap U=U'$ and $X''\cap U=U''$ \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.2.1.6}.
----- French corrections on page IV-2 115 -----
[id 13500]
OLD: (avec les notations de (5.9.6)).
NEW: \textup{(}avec les notations de \textup{(5.9.6))}.
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): érent soit $Z$-pur, et de (3.1.7).

\begin{corollary}[5.10.4]
\label{IV.2.s5.10.4-fr}
Supposons $\mathcal F$ cohérent. Pour que $\mathcal F$ soit $Z$-pur en un
point $x\in X$, il faut et il suffit que
$\operatorname{prof}_{Z_x}(\widetilde{\mathcal F}_x)\geq1$
\textup{(}avec les notations de \textup{(5.9.6))}.
\end{corollary}

Cela résulte aussitôt de (5.10.2) et (5.9.6).

\begin{theorem}[5.10.5]
\EGAReaderTarget{ega4.c04.thm.5-10-5}{IV.2.s5.10.5-fr}
Soient $X$ un préschéma localement noethérien, $Z$ une partie de $X$ stable
par spécialisation, $\mathcal F$ un $\ma

----- English, components/12a_section512_quotient_by_regular_element_through_5127.tex, page IV-2 128 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{128}

  It is
immediate that $\Spec(A/tA)$ is irreducible of dimension $1$,
that $A/tA$ does not satisfy $(\mathrm S_1)$, and that
$\Spec(A)$ is not equidimensional.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[5.12.4]
\label{IV.5.12.4}
Let $A$ be a catenary Noetherian local ring, let $t$ be a regular
element belonging to the maximal ideal of $A$, and let $k\geq1$.
If $A/tA$ satisfies $(\mathrm S_k)$, then so does $A$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For $k=1$ this follows from \sref{IV.3.4.4}, in view of the
interpretation of $(\mathrm S_1)$ in \sref{IV.5.7.5}.  For
$k\geq2$, Hartshorne's criterion \sref{IV.5.10.9} shows that
$A/tA$ is equidimensional, and {\hyperref[IV.5.12.2]{Proposition~5.12.2}} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[5.12.5]
\label{IV.5.12.5}
Let $A$ be a catenary Noetherian local ring, let $t$ be a regular
element of $A$ belonging to its maximal ideal, and let $k\geq0$.
If $A/tA$ is reduced and equidimensional and satisfies
$(\mathrm R_k)$, then $A$ has these same three properties.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We already know from \sref{IV.3.4.6} that $A$ is reduced, and
{\hyperref[IV.5.12.2]{Proposition~5.12.2}}, applied with $k=1$, shows that $A$ is
equidimensional.  Put
\[
  X=\Spec(A),\qquad X_0=V(t)=\Spec(A/tA),
\]
and let $Z$ be the set of points at which $X$ is not regular.
Every specialization of a point of $Z$ belongs to $Z$
\sref[0]{0.17.3.2}.  On the other hand, if $x\in X_0$ and $X_0$
is regular at $x$, then $X$ is regular at $x$
\sref[0]{0.17.1.8}.  Consequently the nonregular locus $Z'_0$
of $X_0$ contains $Z_0=Z\cap X_0$.  The hypothesis gives
\[
  k<\codim(Z'_0,X_0)\leq\codim(Z_0,X_0).
\]
Since $A$ is equidimensional and catenary, {\hyperref[IV.5.12.1]{Proposition~5.12.1}}
gives
\[
  \codim(Z_0,X_0)\leq\codim(Z,X).
\]
Thus $X$ satisfies $(\mathrm R_k)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[5.12.6]
\label{IV.5.12.6}
If in {\hyperref[IV.5.12.5]{Proposition~5.12.5}} one assumes only that $A/tA$ is reduced
and satisfies $(\mathrm R_k)$, it need not follow that $A$
satisfies $(\mathrm R_k)$.  For example, let $k$ be a field and
let $P(U,V)\in k[U,V]$ be irreducible, with the curve $\Gamma$
defined by $(P)$ in $\Spec(k[U,V])$ singular at the maximal ideal
$(U)+(V)$.  One may take, for example, $P(U,V)=U(U^2+V^2)+(U^2\setmin V^2)$,
which defines a cubic with a double point.  Let
$B=k[T,U,V,W]$, let $C$ be the local ring of $B$ at the maximal
ideal $BT+BU+BV+BW$, and put
\[
  \mathfrak p=CW+CP(U\setmin T,V),\qquad \mathfrak q=CU.
\]
Consider the local ring $A=C/\mathfrak p\mathfrak q$.
Geometrically, the corresponding closed subscheme $X$ of affine
$4$-space is the union of a hyperplane $H$ and a
$2$-dimensional ``cylinder'' $L$ with base $\Gamma$; the
``singular line'' $Y$ of $L$ is not contained in $H$, and $A$ is
the local ring of $X$ at the point $x=Y\cap H$.

Let $t$ be the image of $T$ in $A$.  The scheme
$\Spec(A/tA)$ has $x$ as its only singular point; its local ring
there is $A/tA$, which is reduced and has dimension $2$.  By
contrast, the generic point $y$ of $Y$, defined by the image in
$A$ of $C(U\setmin T)+CV+CW$,
is a singular point of $X$, and $\sh O_{X,y}$ has dimension $1$.
Thus $A/tA$ is reduced and satisfies $(\mathrm R_1)$, whereas
$A$ does not satisfy $(\mathrm R_1)$.
\end{remark}
----- English, components/12a_section512_quotient_by_regular_element_through_5127.tex, page IV-2 129 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{129}

\begin{corollary}[5.12.7]
\label{IV.5.12.7}
Let $A$ be a catenary Noetherian local ring and let $t$ be a
regular element of $A$ belonging to its maximal ideal.  If
$A/tA$ is a domain and is integrally closed, then so is $A$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By Serre's criterion \sref{IV.5.8.6} and the fact that $A$ is
local, it is enough to prove that $\Spec(A)$ satisfies
$(\mathrm S_2)$ and $(\mathrm R_1)$.  The hypothesis on $A/tA$
implies $(\mathrm R_1)$ for $A$ by {\hyperref[IV.5.12.5]{Proposition~5.12.5}} and
$(\mathrm S_2)$ for $A$ by {\hyperref[IV.5.12.4]{Corollary~5.12.4}}.
\end{proof}
----- English, components/12b_section512_hironaka_lemma_and_regular_sequences.tex, page IV-2 129 -----
\begin{proposition}[5.12.8]
\label{IV.5.12.8}
{\normalfont(Hironaka's lemma).}
Let $A$ be a reduced, equidimensional, catenary Noetherian local
ring.  Suppose in addition that, for every minimal prime ideal
$\mathfrak q_i$ of $A$, the ring
$B_i=A/\mathfrak q_i$ has the property that $B_i^{(1)}$
\textup{(notation of \sref{IV.5.10.17})} is a finite $B_i$-algebra.  This
last condition holds, for example, when $A$ has either property
{\rm(i)} or property {\rm(ii)} of \sref{IV.5.11.6}.

Let $t\in A$ be a non-zero-divisor satisfying the following
conditions:
\begin{enumerate}[{\rm(i)}]
  \item The $A$-module $A/tA$ has a unique nonembedded associated
  prime ideal $\mathfrak p$.  \textup{(It necessarily has height $1$ by
  the Hauptidealsatz \sref[0]{0.16.3.2}, but it is not assumed to
  be the only associated prime of $A/tA$.)}
  \item The ideal $\mathfrak pA_{\mathfrak p}$ is generated by
  $t/1$.
  \item The ring $A/\mathfrak p$ is integrally closed.
\end{enumerate}
Then $A$ is a domain and is integrally closed, and
$\mathfrak p=tA$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $X=\Spec(A)$ and $Z=V(tA)$.  Condition {\rm(i)} says that
$Z$ is irreducible, with generic point corresponding to
$\mathfrak p$.  By {\rm(ii)}, the maximal ideal of the
Noetherian local ring $A_{\mathfrak p}$ is generated by one
element.  Thus $A_{\mathfrak p}$ is a \emph{discrete valuation ring}
with uniformizer $t/1$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg\`ebre commutative},
chap.~VI, \S3, n\textsuperscript{o}~6, prop.~9), and
$A_{\mathfrak p}/tA_{\mathfrak p}$, its residue field, has length
$1$ as an $A_{\mathfrak p}$-module.

It follows from \sref{IV.3.4.2} that $Z$ is contained in \emph{only one}
irreducible component, say $X_i$, of $X$.  For every other
irreducible component $X_j$ one consequently has
\[
  \dim(X_j\cap Z)<\dim(Z)=\dim(X_i\cap Z).
\]
Since $t$ is not a zero divisor, it belongs to none of the
minimal prime ideals $\mathfrak q_j$, and hence $\codim(X_i\cap Z,X_i)
=\codim(X_j\cap Z,X_j)=1$
for every $j\ne i$ \sref{IV.5.1.8}.  But $A$ is
biequidimensional; the displayed strict inequality would
therefore force $\dim(X_i)\ne\dim(X_j)$
\sref[0]{0.14.3.3.1}, a contradiction.  Hence $A$ has \emph{only one
minimal prime}.  Since it is reduced, $A$ is a \emph{domain}.

Let $A^{(1)}$ have the meaning of \sref{IV.5.10.17}.  It is a
finite $A$-algebra and therefore a Noetherian semilocal ring.
Every nonembedded associated prime $\mathfrak r$ of
$A^{(1)}/tA^{(1)}$ has height $1$ by the Hauptidealsatz.  Its
contraction $\mathfrak r\cap A$ also has height $1$
\sref{IV.5.10.17},~(iv), and, since it contains $tA$, condition
{\rm(i)} forces
\[
  \mathfrak r\cap A=\mathfrak p.
\]

Let $A'$ be the integral closure of $A$ and put
$S=A\setmin\mathfrak p$.  The integral closure of $A_{\mathfrak p}$ is
$S^{-1}A'$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg\`ebre commutative}, chap.~V,
\S1, n\textsuperscript{o}~5, prop.~17); it equals $A_{\mathfrak p}$ because this
ring is a discrete valuation ring.  Consequently there is \emph{only
one} prime $\mathfrak p'$ of $A'$ above $\mathfrak p$
(\emph{loc.\ cit.}, \S2, n\textsuperscript{o}~1, lemma~1), and \emph{therefore} only one
prime $\mathfrak p^{(1)}$ of $A^{(1)}$ above $\mathfrak p$.
Moreover, $A_{\mathfrak p}=A'_{\mathfrak p'}
=A^{(1)}_{\mathfrak p^{(1)}}$.
----- English, components/12b_section512_hironaka_lemma_and_regular_sequences.tex, page IV-2 130 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{130}

The ring $A^{(1)}$ satisfies $(\mathrm S_2)$
\sref{IV.5.10.17},~(i).  Since $t$ is a non-zero-divisor on
$A^{(1)}$, the quotient $A^{(1)}/tA^{(1)}$
has no embedded associated primes \sref{IV.5.7.7}.  Thus
$\mathfrak p^{(1)}$ is its only associated prime, so
$tA^{(1)}$ is $\mathfrak p^{(1)}$-primary.  Equivalently,
$tA^{(1)}$ is the inverse image in $A^{(1)}$ of $tA^{(1)}_{\mathfrak p^{(1)}}=tA_{\mathfrak p}$,
which is the maximal ideal of $A_{\mathfrak p}$ by {\rm(ii)}.
It follows that $tA^{(1)}=\mathfrak p^{(1)}$
is prime in $A^{(1)}$.

The domain $A^{(1)}/\mathfrak p^{(1)}$ is finite over
$A/\mathfrak p$ and has the same fraction field, namely the
residue field of
$A^{(1)}_{\mathfrak p^{(1)}}=A_{\mathfrak p}$.  Condition
{\rm(iii)} therefore gives $A^{(1)}/\mathfrak p^{(1)}=A/\mathfrak p$.
Hence $A^{(1)}=A+\mathfrak p^{(1)}=A+tA^{(1)}$.
Since $t$ lies in the Jacobson radical of the Noetherian
semilocal ring $A^{(1)}$, Nakayama's lemma yields
$A^{(1)}=A$.  Thus $\mathfrak p^{(1)}=\mathfrak p=tA$.
Finally, $A$ is catenary and
$A/tA=A/\mathfrak p$ is a domain and integrally closed by
{\rm(iii)}.  {\hyperref[IV.5.12.7]{Corollary~5.12.7}} shows that $A$ is integrally
closed.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[5.12.9]
\label{IV.5.12.9}
The conclusion of Hironaka's lemma fails without the hypothesis
that $A$ is equidimensional.  Let $k$ be a field, put
\[
  B=k[X,Y,Z],\qquad
  \mathfrak r_1=BZ,\qquad
  \mathfrak r_2=BX+BY,\qquad
  C=B/\mathfrak r_1\mathfrak r_2.
\]
Let $\mathfrak m=BX+BY+BZ$, let
$\mathfrak n=\mathfrak m/\mathfrak r_1\mathfrak r_2$ be its
image in $C$, and let $A=C_{\mathfrak n}$.  Finally, let $t_0$
be the image of $Y+Z$ in $C$, and let $t$ be its image in $A$.
Thus $\Spec(C)$ is the union of a plane $P$ and a line $D$ not
contained in $P$, while $\Spec(C/t_0C)$ is a line $D'$ contained
in $P$ and passing through $D\cap P$.

The ring $A$ is reduced and catenary, since it is a quotient of a
regular ring, and its minimal prime ideals $\mathfrak q_1$ and
$\mathfrak q_2$ are the images of $\mathfrak r_1$ and
$\mathfrak r_2$.  The $A$-module $A/tA$ has a unique
nonembedded associated prime $\mathfrak p$, the image of
$BY+BZ$; the ideal $\mathfrak pA_{\mathfrak p}$ is generated by
$t/1$, and $A/\mathfrak p\simeq k[X]$.  Nevertheless, $A$ is
not a domain.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[5.12.10]
\label{IV.5.12.10}
Let $A$ be a Noetherian local domain satisfying one of the
following hypotheses:
\begin{enumerate}[{\rm a)}]
  \item $A$ is a quotient of a regular ring;
  \item $A$ is universally catenary and universally Japanese.
\end{enumerate}
Let $(x_i)_{1\leq i\leq n}$ be a sequence of $n$ elements of
$A$, and put $\mathfrak J=x_1A+\ldots+x_nA$.
Suppose that:
\begin{enumerate}[{\rm(i)}]
  \item $A/\mathfrak J$ has a unique nonembedded associated
  prime $\mathfrak p$, and $\mathfrak p$ has height $n$;
  \item the maximal ideal $\mathfrak pA_{\mathfrak p}$ of $A_{\mathfrak p}$
  is generated by the $x_i/1$ \textup{(so $A_{\mathfrak p}$ is a
  \emph{regular} local ring of dimension $n$)};
  \item $A/\mathfrak p$ is integrally closed.
\end{enumerate}
Then, for every $i$ with $0\leq i\leq n$, the quotient $A/(x_1A+\ldots+x_iA)$
is integrally closed and has dimension $\dim(A)\setmin i$.  In
particular, $\mathfrak J$ is prime and equals $\mathfrak p$, the
ring $A$ is integrally closed, and $(x_i)_{1\leq i\leq n}$ is an
$A$-regular sequence.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
We argue by induction on $n$.  The assertion is trivial for
$n=0$ and reduces to Hironaka's lemma \sref{IV.5.12.8} for $n=1$, so
suppose $n\geq2$.
Put $\mathfrak J'=x_1A+\ldots+x_{n-1}A$,
and let $\mathfrak q$ be a prime minimal among those containing
$\mathfrak J'$.  Then
$\operatorname{ht}(\mathfrak q)\leq n\setmin 1$
\sref[0]{0.16.3.1}.  We first show that
$\mathfrak q\subset\mathfrak p$.  If $\mathfrak p'$ is minimal
among the primes containing $\mathfrak q+x_nA$, then
$\mathfrak p'/\mathfrak q$ has height $1$ in $A/\mathfrak q$ by the
Hauptidealsatz \sref[0]{0.16.3.2}, hence $\mathfrak p'$ has height
$\leq n$ since $A$ is catenary \sref[0]{0.16.1.4}; but since it
contains $\mathfrak J$, it is necessarily equal to $\mathfrak p$, whence
$\mathfrak q\subset\mathfrak p$.
----- English, components/13a_section513_direct_limits_through_integral_closure.tex, page IV-2 130 -----
\subsection{Properties preserved under passage to a direct limit}
\label{subsection:IV.5.13}

\begin{enumerate}

immergé $\mathfrak p$ \textup{(nécessairement de hauteur 1 en vertu du
Hauptidealsatz

=================== PAGE IV-2 149 ===================
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-6.tex, page IV-2 148 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{148}
of $k$ \sref{IV.4.3.1}. Nevertheless, if $k'=k(a^{1/p})$, then $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}\otimes_k k'$ is not normal: in $k'[\mathrm{S}]$ one has $\mathrm{S}^p-a=(\mathrm{S}\setmin a^{1/p})^p$,
and $\mathrm{X}'$ is therefore isomorphic to $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A}')$, where $\mathrm{A}'=k'[\mathrm{S}][t']$ and $t'$ is a root of $\mathrm{T}^2-\mathrm{S}^p$. The ring $\mathrm{A}'$ is not integrally closed: the element $t''=t'/\mathrm{S}$ of its field of fractions $\mathrm{K}'=k'(\mathrm{S})[t']$ satisfies the integral-dependence equation $t''^2-\mathrm{S}^{p-2}=0$
over $\mathrm{A}'$, but does not belong to $\mathrm{A}'$. In classical terminology, the ``\emph{genus}'' over $k'$ of the field $\mathrm{K}'$ of rational functions of $\mathrm{X}'$ is \emph{strictly smaller} than that of $\mathrm{K}$ over $k$.

(ii) As recalled in (i), it follows from \sref{IV.4.6.1} that when $\mathrm{X}$ is not a geometrically reduced algebraic prescheme over $k$, there exist finite radicial extensions $k'$ of $k$ such that $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}\otimes_k k'$ is not reduced. It is interesting to give an example of this fact where $\mathrm{X}$ is a \emph{regular} scheme over $k$, such that $k$ is \emph{algebraically closed} in the field $\mathrm{K}$ of rational functions of $\mathrm{X}$. Let $k$ be a field of characteristic $p>0$, in which there exist two elements $a$, $b$ forming a $p$-free family over $k^p$. Denoting again by $\mathrm{B}$ the ring $k[\mathrm{S},\mathrm{T}]$, consider this time the polynomial $\mathrm{P}(\mathrm{S},\mathrm{T})=\mathrm{T}^p-a\mathrm{S}^p-b$; as $a\mathrm{S}^p+b$ is not a $p$-th power in $k(\mathrm{S})$, $\mathrm{P}$ is irreducible as a polynomial of $k(\mathrm{S})[\mathrm{T}]$, and the scheme $\mathrm{X}_0=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}/\mathrm{PB})$ is therefore an \emph{integral} affine curve over $k$, whose field of rational functions is $\mathrm{K}=k(\mathrm{S})[t]$, where $t$ is a root of $\mathrm{P}$; let us show that $k$ is \emph{algebraically closed} in $\mathrm{K}$. Suppose indeed that $\mathrm{K}$ contains an element $z$ algebraic over $k$ and not in $k$; one would then also have $z\notin k(\mathrm{S})$, so $[k(\mathrm{S})[z]:k(\mathrm{S})]>1$; as $[\mathrm{K}:k(\mathrm{S})]=p$, one would have $\mathrm{K}=k(\mathrm{S})[z]$, and as $\mathrm{K}$ is radicial over $k(\mathrm{S})$, one would have $z^p\in k(\mathrm{S})$, so $c=z^p\in k$ since $z$ is algebraic over $k$; but then one would have $t^p=a\mathrm{S}^p+b\in k^p(\mathrm{S}^p)(c)$ hence $a$ and $b$ would belong to $k^p(c)$, which is absurd. Let $\mathrm{X}$ then be the \emph{normalisation} of the curve $\mathrm{X}_0$ in $\mathrm{K}$, which is therefore a normal curve (and hence \emph{regular}) over $k$. If $k'=k(a^{1/p},b^{1/p})$, it is clear that $a\mathrm{S}^p+b$ is a $p$-th power in $k'(\mathrm{S})$, hence $\mathrm{K}\otimes_k k'$ is \emph{not reduced}, nor \emph{a fortiori} the scheme $\mathrm{X}\otimes_k k'$.
\end{remarks}

\begin{definition}[6.7.6]
\label{IV.6.7.6}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{X}$ a $k$-prescheme locally Noetherian. We say that $\mathrm{X}$ is \textup{geometrically regular at a point $x$ (resp.\ has the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$ geometric) at a point $x$, resp.\ is geometrically normal at a point $x$)} if, \emph{for every} finite extension $k'$ of $k$, $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}_{(k')}=\mathrm{X}\otimes_k k'$ is regular \textup{(resp.}\ has the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$\textup{, resp.}\ is normal) at every point $x'$ of $\mathrm{X}'$ whose projection in $\mathrm{X}$ is $x$. We say that $\mathrm{X}$ is \emph{geometrically regular} \textup{(resp.}\ has the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$ \emph{geometric}, \textup{or equivalently} is geometrically regular in codimension $\leqslant n$)\textup{, resp.}\ is \emph{geometrically normal}) if $\mathrm{X}$ is geometrically regular \textup{(resp.}\ has the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$ geometric\textup{, resp.}\ is geometrically normal) at every point.
\end{definition}

We say that an algebra $\mathrm{A}$ over $k$ is a \emph{geometrically regular ring} (resp.\ \emph{geometrically normal}, resp.\ \emph{geometrically reduced}, resp.\ \emph{having the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$ geometric}) if $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ has the same property.

If $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{K})$, where $\mathrm{K}$ is an extension of $k$, then saying that $\mathrm{X}$ is \emph{geometrically regular}, or \emph{geometrically normal}, or \emph{geometrically reduced}, amounts to saying that $\mathrm{K}$ is a \emph{separable} extension of $k$: this follows from \sref{IV.4.6.1} and the fact that if $\mathrm{K}$ is a separable extension of $k$ and $k'$ a finite extension of $k$, $\mathrm{K}\otimes_k k'$ is composed of a direct product of a finite number of fields (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.}, chap.~VIII, \S~7, n\textsuperscript{o}~3, cor.~1 of th.~1).
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-6.tex, page IV-2 149 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{149}

\begin{proposition}[6.7.7]
\label{IV.6.7.7}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{X}$ a $k$-prescheme locally Noetherian, $x$ a point of $\mathrm{X}$; denote by $\boldsymbol{Q}(k')$ the relation ``$k'$ is an extension of $k$, and $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X}_{(k')},x')$ is true for every point $x'$ of $\mathrm{X}_{(k')}$ whose projection in $\mathrm{X}$ is $x$'', where $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{Z},z)$ is one of the properties \emph{a)}, \emph{b)}, \emph{c)} of the statement \sref{IV.6.7.4}. Then the following properties are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\emph{\alph*)}]
  \item $\boldsymbol{Q}(k')$ is true for every finite extension $k'$ of $k$.
  \item $\boldsymbol{Q}(k')$ is true for every finite radicial extension $k'$ of $k$.
  \item $\boldsymbol{Q}(k')$ is true for every extension of finite type $k'$ of $k$.
\end{enumerate}
Suppose in addition that $\mathrm{X}$ is locally of finite type over $k$; then the three preceding properties are also equivalent to the following:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\emph{\alph*)}]
\setcounter{enumi}{3}
  \item $\boldsymbol{Q}(k')$ is true for every extension $k'$ of $k$.
  \item $\boldsymbol{Q}(k')$ is true for \textup{a} perfect extension $k'$ of $k$.
  \item $\boldsymbol{Q}(k')$ is true for every extension $k'=k^{p^{-s}}$, where $p$ is the characteristic exponent of $k$, and $s>0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
To prove the equivalence of \emph{a)}, \emph{b)} and \emph{c)}, it clearly suffices to show that \emph{b)} implies \emph{c)}. Let $\mathrm{K}$ be an extension of finite type of $k$, which is then the field of fractions of a $k$-algebra of finite type $\mathrm{A}$; set $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$. We know \sref{IV.4.6.6} that there exists a finite and radicial extension $k'$ of $k$ such that $\mathrm{Y}'=(\mathrm{Y}\otimes_k k')_{\mathrm{red}}$ is separable over $k'$; if $\eta'$ is the generic point of $\mathrm{Y}'$, $\mathrm{K}'=\boldsymbol{k}(\eta')$ is a separable extension of $k'$ by \sref{IV.4.6.1}. This being so, $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X}_{(k')},x')$ is true by hypothesis for every point $x'$ of $\mathrm{X}_{(k')}$ above $x$, so it follows from \sref{IV.6.7.4} that $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K}')},x'')$ is true for every point $x''$ of $\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K}')}$ above $x$, since $\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K}')}=(\mathrm{X}_{(k')})_{(\mathrm{K}')}$, $x''$ is above a point $x'$ of $\mathrm{X}_{(k')}$, itself above $x$, and $\mathrm{K}'$ is separable over $k'$. But we also have $\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K}')}=(\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K})})_{(\mathrm{K}')}$ and for every $x_0\in\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K})}$ above $x$, there exists $x''\in\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K}')}$ above $x_0$; it therefore follows from \sref{IV.6.7.4} that $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K})},x_0)$ is true.

Suppose now that $\mathrm{X}$ is locally of finite type over $k$, so that for \emph{every} extension $\mathrm{K}$ of $k$, $\mathrm{X}_{(\mathrm{K})}$ is locally Noetherian. As a radicial extension of $k$ is isomorphic to a sub-extension of any perfect extension of $k$, it follows immediately from \sref{IV.6.7.4} that \emph{e)} implies \emph{f)}; also, every finite radicial extension of $k$ is contained in an extension $k^{p^{-s}}\subset k^{p^{-\infty}}$, so \emph{f)} implies \emph{b)}; \emph{d)} trivially implies \emph{e)}, and finally \emph{e)} implies \emph{d)}. Indeed, if $\mathrm{K}$ is any extension \emph{whatever} of $k$, there exists an extension $\mathrm{K}'$ of $k$ containing (up to $k$-isomorphisms) $k'$ and $\mathrm{K}$; since $\mathrm{K}'$ is a \emph{separable} extension of $k'$, one deduces from \sref{IV.6.7.4} that $\boldsymbol{Q}(\mathrm{K}')$ is true, then that $\boldsymbol{Q}(\mathrm{K})$ is true, reasoning as in the first part of the proof. It therefore suffices to prove that \emph{b) implies e)}. We shall take $k'=k^{p^{-\infty}}$ in \emph{e)}.

The question being local on $\mathrm{X}$, one can furthermore limit oneself to the case where $\mathrm{X}$ is affine and of finite type over $k$, by replacing $\mathrm{X}$ by a neighbourhood of $x$; let then $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$, where $\mathrm{B}$ is a $k$-algebra of finite type; furthermore, by definition of the property $\boldsymbol{P}$ in the cases considered, one can also replace $\mathrm{X}$ by the local scheme of $\mathrm{X}$ at the point $x$, hence suppose that $\mathrm{B}$ is a \emph{local} Noetherian ring. Set $\mathrm{B}'=\mathrm{B}\otimes_k k'$; $k'$ is the inductive limit of its \emph{finite} sub-extensions $k'_\lambda$ and if one sets $\mathrm{B}'_\lambda=\mathrm{B}\otimes_k k'_\lambda$, the morphism $f_\lambda:\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}')\to\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}'_\lambda)$ is a \emph{homeomorphism} of $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}')$ \emph{onto} $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}'_\lambda)$ for every $\lambda$;
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-6.tex, page IV-2 150 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{150}

indeed $f_\lambda$ is bijective by virtue of \sref[I]{I.3.5.2}, \sref[I]{I.3.5.7} and \sref[I]{I.3.5.8}; on the other hand, it is closed by \sref[II]{II.6.1.10}. One can now apply \sref{IV.5.13.6} which proves (by virtue of the hypothesis \emph{b)}) that \emph{b)} implies \emph{e)} when $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{Z},z)$ is the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$ at the point $z$. The case where $\boldsymbol{P}$ is the property of being regular (i.e.\ of having the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$ for every $n$) follows trivially. Finally, taking into account Serre's normality criterion \sref{IV.5.8.6}, \emph{b)} implies \emph{e)} further when $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{Z},z)$ is the property of being normal at the point $z$, since this is equivalent to saying that $\mathrm{Z}$ has at the point $z$ the properties $(\mathrm{S}_2)$ and $(\mathrm{R}_1)$: one applies what precedes for $n=1$, and \sref{IV.6.7.2},~(ii) for $n=2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[6.7.8]
\label{IV.6.7.8}
Let $k$ be a field; for a $k$-prescheme locally Noetherian $\mathrm{X}$ and a point $x\in\mathrm{X}$, denote by $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X},x)$ any of the following properties:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item $\operatorname{coprof}(\sh{O}_x)\leqslant n$.
  \item $\sh{O}_x$ is a Cohen-Macaulay ring.
  \item $\mathrm{X}$ satisfies the property $(\mathrm{S}_n)$ at the point $x$.
  \item $\mathrm{X}$ is geometrically regular at the point $x$.
  \item $\mathrm{X}$ has the property $(\mathrm{R}_n)$ geometric at the point $x$.
  \item $\mathrm{X}$ is geometrically normal at the point $x$.
  \item $\mathrm{X}$ is geometrically reduced (\textup{i.e.}\ separable) at the point $x$.
\end{enumerate}
Let $k'$ be an extension of $k$; one supposes either that $k'$ is of finite type, or that $\mathrm{X}$ is locally of finite type over $k$, so that $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}\otimes_k k'$ is locally Noetherian. Let $x'$ be a point of $\mathrm{X}'$ whose projection in $\mathrm{X}$ is $x$. Then, for $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X},x)$ to be true, it is necessary and sufficient that $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X}',x')$ be so.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
This has already been seen for property (vii) \sref{IV.4.6.11}, and for properties (i), (ii), and (iii) \sref{IV.6.7.1}. For (iv), (v), and (vi), this follows from \sref{IV.6.7.7}: the necessity follows from the equivalence of criteria \emph{a)} and \emph{c)}, and from the equivalence of \emph{c)} and \emph{d)} when $\mathrm{X}$ is locally of finite type over $k$. To prove sufficiency, let $k''$ be a finite radicial extension of $k$; one may regard $k'$ and $k''$ as subextensions of an extension $\mathrm{K}$ of $k$. Set $\mathrm{X}''=\mathrm{X}\otimes_k k''$ and $\mathrm{X}_0=\mathrm{X}\otimes_k\mathrm{K}$, and note that, since $k''$ is radicial over $k$, there is only \emph{one} point $x''$ of $\mathrm{X}''$ above $x$ \sref[I]{I.3.5.7} and \sref[I]{I.3.5.8}. Let $x_0$ be any point of $\mathrm{X}_0$ above $x'$. If $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X}',x')$ is true, then so is $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X}_0,x_0)$ by \sref{IV.6.7.7},~\emph{c)} and \emph{d)} (if $k'$ is of finite type over $k$, one may suppose the same of $\mathrm{K}$; if $\mathrm{X}$ is locally of finite type over $k$, then $\mathrm{X}'$ is locally of finite type over $k'$). It follows from \sref{IV.6.7.4} that $\boldsymbol{Q}(k'')$ is true (with the notation of \sref{IV.6.7.7}), and hence $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{X},x)$ is true by \sref{IV.6.7.7},~\emph{b)}.
\end{proof}

  \item of Cohen-Macaulay at the point $x$;

  \item $(\mathrm{S}_n)$ at the point $x$;

if $f$ is flat at the point $x$, and if in addition the corresponding property $\boldsymbol{P}(f^{-1}(f(x)),x)$ \textnormal{(}notation of \sref{IV.6.7.8}\textnormal{)} is true.

We say that $f$ is \emph{smooth} at the point $x$ if it is locally of finite presentation in a neighbourhood of $x$ and if it is regular at the point $x$. We say respectively that $f$ is a morphism: of coprofundity $\leqslant n$, of Cohen-Macaulay, $(\mathrm{S}_n)$, regular, $(\mathrm{R}_n)$, normal, reduced, smooth, if it has the corresponding property at every point of $\mathrm{X}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[6.8.2]
\label{IV.6.8.2}
Let $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$ be two locally Noetherian preschemes, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ a morphism, $x$ a point of $\mathrm{X}$. Denote by $\boldsymbol{M}(f,x)$ one of the properties \textup{(i)} to \textup{(vii)} of the definition \sref{IV.6.8.1}, or the property of being smooth at the point $x$. Let $\mathrm{Y}'$ be a locally Noetherian prescheme, $g:\mathrm{Y}'\to\mathrm{Y}$ a morphism, $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}\times_\mathrm{Y}\mathrm{Y}'$, $f'=f_{(\mathrm{Y}')}:\mathrm{X}'\to\mathrm{Y}'$. Suppose that $f$ or $g$ is locally of finite type. Then, for every $x'\in\mathrm{X}'$ above $x$, the property $\boldsymbol{M}(f,x)$ implies $\boldsymbol{M}(f',x')$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $y=f(x)$, $y'=f'(x')$; by transitivity of fibres \sref[I]{I.3.6.4}, we have $f'^{-1}(y')=f^{-1}(y)\otimes_{\boldsymbol{k}(y)}\boldsymbol{k}(y')$. Since either $f^{-1}(y)$ is locally of finite type over $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$, or $\boldsymbol{k}(y')$ is of finite type over $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$ \sref[I]{I.6.4.11}, it follows from \sref{IV.6.7.8} that the properties $\boldsymbol{P}(f^{-1}(y),x)$ and $\boldsymbol{P}(f'^{-1}(y'),x')$ are equivalent; furthermore, if $f$ is flat at the point $x$, $f'$ is flat at the point $x'$ \sref{IV.2.1.4}, which proves the proposition, taking into account \sref{IV.1.4.3},~(iii).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[6.8.3]
\label{IV.6.8.3}
For a morphism $f$ of locally Noetherian preschemes, let $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ be one of the following properties: being Cohen-Macaulay, $(\mathrm{S}_n)$, regular, $(\mathrm{R}_n)$, normal, reduced.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item Let $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$, $\mathrm{Z}$ be three locally Noetherian preschemes, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$, $g:\mathrm{Y}\to\mathrm{Z}$ two morphisms. If $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ and $\boldsymbol{M}(g)$ are true, $\boldsymbol{M}(g\circ f)$ is true.
  \item Conversely, if $f$ is surjective and if $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ and $\boldsymbol{M}(g\circ f)$ are true, then $\boldsymbol{M}(g)$ is true.
  \item Let $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$, $\mathrm{Y}'$ be three locally Noetherian preschemes, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$, $h:\mathrm{Y}'\to\mathrm{Y}$ two morphisms; set $\mathrm{X}'=\mathrm{X}\times_\mathrm{Y}\mathrm{Y}'$, $f'=f_{(\mathrm{Y}')}:\mathrm{X}'\to\mathrm{Y}'$. Suppose that $f$ or $h$ is locally of finite type. If $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ is true, then so is $\boldsymbol{M}(f')$; the converse is true when $h$ is faithfully flat.
\end{enumerate}
The conclusions of \textup{(i)} and \textup{(iii)} are also true when $\boldsymbol{M}$ is the property of being smooth and (in \textup{(iii)}) $h$ is quasi-compact.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) We already know that if $f$ and $g$ are flat, so is $u=g\circ f$, and that if $f$ and $g\circ f$ are flat and $f$ is surjective, then $g$ is flat \sref{IV.2.1.6} and \sref{IV.2.2.13}. On the other hand, for every $z\in\mathrm{Z}$, the morphism $f_z=f\otimes 1_{\boldsymbol{k}(z)}:u^{-1}(z)\to g^{-1}(z)$ is flat (resp.\ faithfully flat if $f$ is) and for every $y\in g^{-1}(z)$, $f_z^{-1}(y)$ is isomorphic to $f^{-1}(y)$ \sref[I]{I.3.6.4}. If $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ and $\boldsymbol{M}(g)$ are true, $\boldsymbol{M}(g\circ f)$ is then true for the cases where $\boldsymbol{M}$ is the property of being Cohen-Macaulay or $(\mathrm{S}_n)$, by virtue of \sref{IV.6.6.1}. On the other hand, let $k'$ be a finite extension of $\boldsymbol{k}(z)$; setting $\mathrm{Y}'_z=g^{-1}(z)\otimes_{\boldsymbol{k}(z)}k'$, $\mathrm{X}'_z=u^{-1}(z)\otimes_{\boldsymbol{k}(z)}k'$, and $f'_z=f_z\otimes 1_{k'}:\mathrm{X}'_z\to\mathrm{Y}'_z$; the morphism $f'_z$
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-6.tex, page IV-2 152 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{152}
is flat (resp.\ faithfully flat) and for every $y'\in\mathrm{Y}'_z$, the fibre $f'^{-1}_z(y')$ is isomorphic to $f^{-1}(y)\otimes_{\boldsymbol{k}(y)}\boldsymbol{k}(y')$, denoting by $y$ the image of $y'$ in $g^{-1}(z)$ \sref[I]{I.3.6.4}. When $\boldsymbol{M}$ is the property of being regular, $(\mathrm{R}_n)$, normal or reduced, the hypothesis that $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ and $\boldsymbol{M}(g)$ are true implies that $\mathrm{Y}'_z$ and each of the fibres $f'^{-1}_z(y')$ for $y'\in\mathrm{Y}'_z$ have the corresponding property among the properties \emph{c)}, \emph{d)}, \emph{e)}, \emph{f)} of \sref{IV.6.6.1}; one deduces from \sref{IV.6.6.1},~(i) that $\mathrm{X}'_z$ has the same property, hence $\boldsymbol{M}(g\circ f)$ is true.

(ii) Conversely, the hypothesis that $\boldsymbol{M}(g\circ f)$ and $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ are true and that $f$ is surjective implies that $\mathrm{Y}'_z$ has the corresponding property by virtue of \sref{IV.6.6.1},~(ii), since $f'_z$ is surjective for every $z$; hence $\boldsymbol{M}(g)$ is true.

(iii) The first assertion follows immediately from \sref{IV.6.8.2}. On the other hand, if $h$ is faithfully flat and $f'$ flat, $f$ is flat \sref{IV.2.4.1}; since, with the notations of \sref{IV.6.8.2}, the properties $\boldsymbol{P}(f^{-1}(y),x)$ and $\boldsymbol{P}(f'^{-1}(y'),x')$ are equivalent \sref{IV.6.7.8}, one sees that $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ and $\boldsymbol{M}(f')$ are then equivalent.

Finally, the last assertion of the proposition follows from \sref{IV.1.4.3},~(iii) and \sref{IV.2.7.1},~(iv).
\end{proof}

\begin{remarks}[6.8.4]
\label{IV.6.8.4}
(i) If $f$ is faithfully flat, $g$ flat and if $g\circ f$ is of coprofundity $\leqslant n$, then $g$ is of coprofundity $\leqslant n$, as follows from the reasoning of \sref{IV.6.6.2}.

(ii) When, in \sref{IV.6.8.1}, one takes for $\mathrm{Y}$ the spectrum of a field $k$, the notions (iv), (v) and (vi) reduce to those defined in \sref{IV.6.7.5}. It is clear that these latter are relative to the base field $k$. The definition \sref{IV.6.8.1} thus leads to saying that a prescheme $\mathrm{X}$ is ``\emph{regular} (resp.\ $(\mathrm{R}_n)$, resp.\ \emph{normal}) \emph{over} $k$'' instead of saying that it is ``geometrically regular (resp.\ $(\mathrm{R}_n)$, resp.\ normal) relative to $k$''; one will avoid confusing this notion with the property of being regular (resp.\ $(\mathrm{R}_n)$, resp.\ normal) which is independent of $k$. One can make in this connection the same remarks as in \sref{IV.4.5.12}.
\end{remarks}

\begin{proposition}[6.8.5]
\label{IV.6.8.5}
Let $k$ be a field, $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$ two $k$-preschemes locally Noetherian, at least one of which is locally of finite type over $k$. For a $k$-prescheme $\mathrm{Z}$, denote by $\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{Z})$ one of the properties \textup{c)} to \textup{f)} of \sref{IV.6.6.1}; the property ``$\boldsymbol{P}(\mathrm{Z})$ geometric'' is then defined in \sref{IV.6.7.6} (resp.\ \sref{IV.4.6.1}). Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
  \item If $\mathrm{X}$ has the property $\boldsymbol{P}$, and $\mathrm{Y}$ the property $\boldsymbol{P}$ geometric, $\mathrm{X}\times_k\mathrm{Y}$ has the property $\boldsymbol{P}$.
  \item If $\mathrm{X}$ and $\mathrm{Y}$ have the property $\boldsymbol{P}$ geometric, so does $\mathrm{X}\times_k\mathrm{Y}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Indeed, let $f:\mathrm{X}\times_k\mathrm{Y}\to\mathrm{X}$, $g:\mathrm{X}\to\operatorname{Spec}(k)$ be the structural morphisms, which are flat by \sref{IV.2.1.2} and \sref{IV.2.1.4}. The hypothesis that $\mathrm{Y}$ has the property $\boldsymbol{P}$ geometric implies by virtue of \sref{IV.6.8.2} that $\boldsymbol{M}(f)$ is true, $\boldsymbol{M}$ being the property \sref{IV.6.8.3} which corresponds to $\boldsymbol{P}$; under the hypothesis (ii), $\boldsymbol{M}(g)$ is also true, hence the assertion (ii) follows from \sref{IV.6.8.3},~(i). As for the assertion (i), it follows directly from \sref{IV.6.6.1},~(i).
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[6.8.6]
\label{IV.6.8.6}
Let $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$ be two locally Noetherian preschemes, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ a morphism locally of finite type, $x$ a point of $\mathrm{X}$, $y=f(x)$. The following properties are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\emph{\alph*)}]
  \item $f$ is smooth at the point $x$.
  \item $f$ is regular at the point $x$.
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-6.tex, page IV-2 153 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{153}
  \item $\sh{O}_x$ is a formally smooth $\sh{O}_y$-algebra \sref[0]{0.19.3.1} for the adic topologies on $\sh{O}_x$ and $\sh{O}_y$.
  \item[\emph{c$'$)}] $\sh{O}_x$ is a formally smooth $\sh{O}_y$-algebra \sref[0]{0.19.3.1} for the discrete topologies on $\sh{O}_x$ and $\sh{O}_y$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The equivalence of \emph{a)} and \emph{b)} follows from the definitions \sref{IV.6.8.1} and from the fact that for locally Noetherian preschemes, morphisms locally of finite type are locally of finite presentation \sref{IV.1.4.2}.

Secondly, for $\sh{O}_x$ to be a formally smooth $\sh{O}_y$-algebra for the adic topologies, it is necessary and sufficient that $\sh{O}_x$ be flat over $\sh{O}_y$ and that $\sh{O}_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_y}\boldsymbol{k}(y)$ be formally smooth over $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$ for its adic topology \sref[0]{0.19.7.1}. But for $\sh{O}_x\otimes_{\sh{O}_y}\boldsymbol{k}(y)$ to be a formally smooth $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$-algebra for its adic topology, it is necessary and sufficient that it be a geometrically regular $\boldsymbol{k}(y)$-algebra \sref[0]{0.19.6.6}; this proves the equivalence of \emph{b)} and \emph{c)}. Finally, to prove the equivalence of \emph{c)} and \emph{c')}, one may reduce to the affine case $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$, $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C})$, where $\mathrm{A}$ is Noetherian and $\mathrm{C}$ is a finite-type Noetherian $\mathrm{A}$-algebra, say $\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]/\mathfrak{J}$. Since $\mathfrak{J}/\mathfrak{J}^2$ is a finitely presented $\mathrm{C}$-module, the equivalence follows from \sref[0]{0.22.6.4} applied to $\mathrm{A}$, $\mathrm{B}=\mathrm{A}[\mathrm{T}_1,\ldots,\mathrm{T}_n]$ and $\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{J}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[6.8.7]
\label{IV.6.8.7}
Let $\mathrm{X}$, $\mathrm{Y}$ be two locally Noetherian preschemes, $f:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ a morphism locally of finite type. Then the set of points $x\in\mathrm{X}$ where $f$ is smooth \textnormal{(}\textup{or} regular\textnormal{)} is open in $\mathrm{X}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Indeed it follows from \sref[0]{0.22.6.5} that the set of $x\in\mathrm{X}$ satisfying the condition \emph{c')} of \sref{IV.6.8.6} is open in $\mathrm{X}$, and we conclude by \sref{IV.6.8.6}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[6.8.8]
\label{IV.6.8.8}
In \sref{IV.17.5.1}, we shall show that the equivalence of \emph{b)} and \emph{c')} in \sref{IV.6.8.6}, as well as the corollary \sref{IV.6.8.7}, remain valid without the Noetherian hypothesis on $\mathrm{X}$ and $\mathrm{Y}$, provided that one restricts to morphisms \emph{locally of finite presentation}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The theorem of generic flatness}
\label{subsection:IV.6.9}

\begin{theorem}[6.9.1]
\label{IV.6.9.1}
Let $\mathrm{Y}$ be a locally Noetherian and integral prescheme, $u:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{Y}$ a morphism of finite type, $\sh{F}$ a coherent $\sh{O}_\mathrm{X}$-Module. There then exists a non-empty open subset $\mathrm{U}$ of $\mathrm{Y}$ such that $\sh{F}|u^{-1}(\mathrm{U})$ is flat over $\mathrm{U}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
One can clearly limit oneself to the case where $\mathrm{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{A})$ is affine; then $\mathrm{X}$ is a finite union of affine opens $\mathrm{X}_i$ of finite type over $\mathrm{Y}$; if, for each $i$, there exists a non-empty open $\mathrm{U}_i$ in $\mathrm{Y}$ such that $\sh{F}|(\mathrm{X}_i\cap u^{-1}(\mathrm{U}_i))$ is flat over $\mathrm{U}_i$, it is clear that taking for $\mathrm{U}$ the intersection of the $\mathrm{U}_i$, we shall have answered the question; one can therefore suppose that $\mathrm{X}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B})$, where $\mathrm{B}$ is an $\mathrm{A}$-algebra of finite type. We then have $\sh{F}=\widetilde{\mathrm{M}}$, where $\mathrm{M}$ is a $\mathrm{B}$-module of finite type; the theorem will follow from the following:
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[6.9.2]
\label{IV.6.9.2}
Let $\mathrm{A}$ be an integral Noetherian ring, $\mathrm{B}$ a finite-type $\mathrm{A}$-algebra, and $\mathrm{M}$ a finite $\mathrm{B}$-module. Then there exists $f\neq 0$ in $\mathrm{A}$ such that $\mathrm{M}_f$ is a free $\mathrm{A}_f$-module.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
----- French corrections on page IV-2 152 -----
[id 14133]

NEW: propriétés \textup{c), d), e) (resp. f))} de \textup{(6.6.1)}. Alors
FRENCH CONTEXT (corrected): éschéma $Z$,
désignons par $\boldsymbol{P}(Z)$ l'une des propriétés \textup{c)} à \textup{f)} de \textup{(6.6.1)}; la propriété
«$\boldsymbol{P}(Z)$ géométrique» est alors définie dans \textup{(6.7.6) (resp. (4.6.1))} lorsque
$\boldsymbol{P}(Z)$ est l'une des propriétés \textup{c), d), e) (resp. f))} de \textup{(6.6.1)}. Alors:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[\textup{(i)}] Si $X$ possède la propriété $\boldsymbol{P}$, et $Y$ la propriété $\boldsymbol{P}$
géométrique, $X\times_kY$ possède la propriété $\boldsymbol{P}$.
\item[\textup{(ii)}] Si $X$ et $Y$ possèdent la propriété $\boldsymbol{P

=================== PAGE IV-2 153 ===================
----- (English page IV-2 152: shown above in this file) -----
----- (English page IV-2 153: shown above in this file) -----
----- English, source_aligned/ega4-6.tex, page IV-2 154 -----
\oldpage[IV-2]{154}
Denote by $\mathrm{K}$ the field of fractions of $\mathrm{A}$; then $\mathrm{B}\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K}$ is an algebra of finite type over $\mathrm{K}$ and $\mathrm{M}\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K}$ is a $(\mathrm{B}\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K})$-module of finite type. We shall reason by induction on the \emph{dimension} $n$ of the support of $\mathrm{M}\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K}$, which is either $\setmin\infty$ or finite and $\geqslant 0$. Suppose first $n=\setmin\infty$, i.e.\ $\mathrm{M}\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K}=0$; if $(m_i)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}$ is a system of generators of the $\mathrm{B}$-module $\mathrm{M}$, there therefore exists $f\neq 0$ in $\mathrm{A}$ such that $fm_i=0$ for $1\leqslant i\leqslant r$; hence $\mathrm{M}_f=0$ and the lemma is true in this case.

Suppose now $n\geqslant 0$. We know that there exists a composition series $\mathrm{M}=\mathrm{M}_1\supset\mathrm{M}_2\supset\ldots\supset\mathrm{M}_q=0$ of the $\mathrm{B}$-module $\mathrm{M}$ such that each quotient $\mathrm{N}_i=\mathrm{M}_i/\mathrm{M}_{i+1}$ is isomorphic to a $\mathrm{B}$-module of the form $\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{p}_i$, where $\mathfrak{p}_i$ is a prime ideal of $\mathrm{B}$ (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~IV, \S~1, n\textsuperscript{o}~4, th.~1). If the theorem is true for each $\mathrm{N}_i$, there exists for each $i$ an $f_i\neq 0$ in $\mathrm{A}$ such that $(\mathrm{N}_i)_{f_i}$ is free over $\mathrm{A}_{f_i}$; setting $f=f_1f_2\ldots f_{q-1}$, it follows that $(\mathrm{N}_i)_f$ is a free $\mathrm{A}_f$-module for $1\leqslant i\leqslant q-1$. But $(\mathrm{N}_i)_f=(\mathrm{M}_i)_f/(\mathrm{M}_{i+1})_f$ \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.1.3.2}, and since an extension of free modules is free, we deduce that $\mathrm{M}_f$ is a free $\mathrm{A}_f$-module.

Replacing $\mathrm{B}$ by $\mathrm{B}/\mathfrak{p}$, where $\mathfrak{p}$ is a prime ideal of $\mathrm{B}$, one can therefore reduce to the case $\mathrm{M}=\mathrm{B}$ \emph{with} $\mathrm{B}$ \emph{integral}. We then know (Bourbaki, \emph{Alg.\ comm.}, chap.~V, \S~3, n\textsuperscript{o}~1, cor.~1 of th.~1) that there exist $g\neq 0$ in $\mathrm{A}$ and elements $t_i$ ($1\leqslant i\leqslant m$) of $\mathrm{B}$, algebraically independent over $\mathrm{A}$, such that $\mathrm{B}_g$ is \emph{integral} over $\mathrm{A}_g[t_1,\ldots,t_m]$. One can replace $\mathrm{A}$ by $\mathrm{A}_g$, $\mathrm{B}$ by $\mathrm{B}_g$ and suppose that $\mathrm{B}$ is integral over $\mathrm{C}=\mathrm{A}[t_1,\ldots,t_m]$, hence a $\mathrm{C}$-module of finite type and \emph{without torsion}. We know furthermore \sref{IV.4.1.2} that the dimension of $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{B}\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K})$ is equal to $m$, hence $m=n$.

This being so, if $h$ is the rank of the $\mathrm{C}$-module without torsion $\mathrm{B}$, there exists a short exact sequence of $\mathrm{C}$-modules
\[
0\to\mathrm{C}^h\to\mathrm{B}\to\mathrm{M}'\to 0
\]
where $\mathrm{M}'$ is a \emph{torsion} $\mathrm{C}$-module of finite type; the support of $\mathrm{M}'$ does not therefore contain the generic point of $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C})$ \sref[I]{I.7.4.6} and hence the support of $\mathrm{M}'\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K}$ does not contain the generic point of $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathrm{C}\otimes_\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K})$ \sref[I]{I.9.1.13.1}; one concludes \sref{IV.4.1.2.1} that its dimension is $<n$. By the induction hypothesis, there exists $f\neq 0$ in $\mathrm{A}$ such that $\mathrm{M}'_f$ is a free $\mathrm{A}_f$-module; moreover $\mathrm{C}^h_f$ is a free $\mathrm{A}_f$-module, and therefore so is $\mathrm{B}_f$, which by \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.1.3.2} is an extension of free modules.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[6.9.3]
\label{IV.6.9.3}
Let $\mathrm{S}$ be a Noetherian prescheme, $u:\mathrm{X}\to\mathrm{S}$ a morphism of finite type, $\sh{F}$ a coherent $\sh{O}_\mathrm{X}$-Module. There then exists a partition $(\mathrm{S}_i)_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}$ of $\mathrm{S}$ into a finite number of locally closed subsets of $\mathrm{S}$, such that, if one still denotes by $\mathrm{S}_i$ the reduced sub-prescheme having $\mathrm{S}_i$ for underlying space, and if one sets $\mathrm{X}_i=\mathrm{X}\times_\mathrm{S}\mathrm{S}_i$, the $\sh{O}_{\mathrm{X}_i}$-Module $\sh{F}_i=\sh{F}\otimes_{\sh{O}_\mathrm{S}}\sh{O}_{\mathrm{S}_i}$ is flat over $\mathrm{S}_i$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
We proceed by Noetherian induction \sref[0\textsubscript{\mdseries I}]{0.2.2.2} on the set of closed subsets $\mathrm{T}$ of $\mathrm{S}$ such that the reduced closed sub-prescheme with underlying space $\mathrm{T}$ satisfies the conclusion of \sref{IV.6.9.3}. One can therefore limit oneself to proving the corollary for $\mathrm{S}$ by supposing that it is true for every reduced closed sub-prescheme whose underlying space is a closed subset distinct from $\mathrm{S}$. Since the morphism $\mathrm{S}_\mathrm{red}\to\mathrm{S}$ is of finite type and surjective, one can replace $\mathrm{S}$ by $\mathrm{S}_\mathrm{red}$, hence suppose $\mathrm{S}$ reduced and non-empty. Since $\mathrm{S}$ is Noetherian, the interior $\mathrm{T}$ of an irreducible component of $\mathrm{S}$ is non-empty, and the induced prescheme on $\mathrm{T}$ is integral; there therefore exists by virtue of \sref{IV.6.9.1} a non-empty open $\mathrm{U}\subset\mathrm{T}$

\begin{corollary}[6.8.7]

Soient $X$, $Y$ deux préschémas localement noethériens, $f:X\to Y$ un

of generators $t'_j$ $(1\leqslant j\leqslant m)$ of $\mathrm{K}'$ over $\mathrm{K}(t_1,\ldots,t_n)$, which one can suppose to be integral
